28 Running title: *Chlamydomonas* triacylglyceride accumulation

- 29
- 30

# 31 **ABSTRACT**

32

33 When the *sta6* (starch-null) strain of the green microalga *Chlamydomonas reinhardtii* is 34 nitrogen-starved in acetate and then "boosted" after two days with additional acetate, the 35 cells become "obese" after eight days, with triacylglyceride-filled lipid bodies filling their 36 cytoplasm and chloroplasts. To assess the transcriptional correlates of this response, 37 *sta6* and the starch-forming *cw15* strain were subjected to RNA-Seq analysis during the 38 two days prior and two days post boost, and the data were compared with published 39 reports using other strains and growth conditions. During the two hours post boost, ~425 40 genes are up-regulated ≥2-fold and ~875 genes are down-regulated ≥2-fold in each 41 strain. Expression of a small subset of "sensitive" genes, encoding enzymes in the 42 glyoxylate and Calvin-Benson cycles, gluconeogenesis, and the pentose phosphate 43 pathway, is responsive to culture conditions and genetic background as well as to boost. 44 Four genes—encoding a diacylglycerol acyltransferase (*DGTT2*), a glycerol-3-P 45 dehydrogenase (*GPD3*), and two candidate lipases (Cre03.g155250 and 46 Cre17.g735600)—are selectively up-regulated in *sta6*. Although the bulk rate of acetate 47 depletion from the medium is not boost-enhanced, three candidate acetate permease-48 encoding genes in the *GPR1\_FUN34\_YaaH* superfamily are boost-up-regulated, and 13 49 of the "sensitive" genes are strongly responsive to the cell's acetate status. A cohort of 50 64 autophagy-related genes is down-regulated by boost. Our results indicate that the 51 boost serves both to avert an autophagy program and to prolong the operation of key

- 52 pathways that shuttle carbon from acetate into storage lipid, the combined outcome
- 53 being enhanced TAG accumulation, notably in *sta6*.
- 54
- 55
- 56 **INTRODUCTION**
- 57
- 58 Eukaryotic microalgae accumulate storage products—polysaccharides (starch,
- 59 (chryso)laminarin) and lipids (triacylglycerides (TAG))—when subjected to growth-
- 60 arresting conditions such as transfer to nitrogen (N)-free media (40,44). When conditions
- 61 improve, the products are broken down and utilized as sources of carbon backbones,
- 62 ATP, and reductant. Since TAG represents a potential feedstock for liquid transportation
- 63 fuel (26, 44, 55, 69), much recent research has explored the molecular and cellular
- 64 parameters associated with TAG biosynthesis.
- 65
- 66 The green microalga *Chlamydomonas reinhardtii* has been the subject of many of these 67 studies since it has a rich history of genetic and biochemical analysis (23), a well-68 annotated genome (43), powerful molecular-genetic tools (23), and a strong starch/TAG 69 response to N-deprivation in wild-type strains (8, 9, 30, 45-47). Of particular interest has 70 been the mutant strain *sta6*, deleted for the gene encoding an ADP-glucose 71 pyrophosphorylase subunit (6, 73) and hence incapable of starch formation. In most 72 studies, *sta6* produces more TAG than starch-forming strains such as *cw15* (6, 15, 16, 73 19, 30, 35, 36, 53, 58, 66, 68, 71), apparently in large part because it assembles TAG-74 filled lipid bodies (LBs) in both the chloroplast and the cytoplasm whereas starch-forming 75 strains produce only cytoplasmic LBs (19). When provisioned with a "boost" of additional 76 acetate, moreover, *sta6* proceeds to become obese such that it floats when centrifuged

77 (19). The boost also enhances LB formation in *cw15*, but the cells fail to achieve obesity 78 and do not float (19).

79

80 Here we report studies on gene-expression patterns during the path to obesity. The 81 Merchant/Pellegrini and Los Alamos laboratories recently generated and analyzed RNA-82 Seq transcriptomes of *cw15*, *sta6*, and several complemented *sta6* strains during two 83 days of N-starvation (048 h NF) (6). In collaboration with these groups, the 84 Goodenough lab generated a second pair of transcriptomes using *cw15* and *sta6*, 85 tracing 048 h NF gene expression patterns under a different set of culture conditions 86 and taking the time course out to 96 h NF, with an intervening acetate boost. Analysis of 87 these data was deeply informed by cross-comparisons with the Blaby *et al.* (6) data. 88 Three additional RNA-Seq studies of wild-type strains (8, 45, 48) were also considered. 89 90 By consolidating these data, it has been possible to identify "robust" biochemical 91 pathways, like starch, fatty-acid, and TAG biosynthesis, wherein patterns of expression 92 of the relevant genes are largely concordant regardless of genetic background or culture 93 conditions, thereby calling attention to the few exceptional cases. Also identified are 94 "sensitive" genes, encoding products operating in several pathways that are influenced 95 by on-going carbon flux; their expression is coordinated but varies within strains and 96 conditions, suggesting that they play a role in monitoring and responding to N-depletion 97 in particular biosynthetic/metabolic contexts. We propose that the several enzymes that 98 are differentially expressed in *sta6*, combined with a glucose-6-P "backflow," participate 99 in generating the chloroplast LBs of this starchless strain. We also propose that the 100 acetate boost deters an autophagy pathway that compromises maximal TAG 101 accumulation.

102

#### 104 **MATERIALS AND METHODS**

105

106 **Strains** The *sta6* strain (CC-4348, Chlamydomonas Center) is flagella-less, cell wall-107 less, and carries an insertional deletion of the *STA6* gene encoding the small subunit of 108 ADP-glucose pyrophosphorylase (6, 72) essential for starch biosynthesis. Blaby *et al.* (6) 109 document that the *sta6* deletion extends into the neighboring *RBO1* gene and that its 110 contiguous orthologue, *RBO2*, is also attenuated in expression. The *cw15* strain, CC-111 4349, was considered the clonal parent to the *sta6* strain; however, recent genomic 112 analyses (6) indicate that it is not the *sta6* parent since it is the opposite mating type and 113 carries distinctive SNPs. While its origin is unclear, its flagellar and wall phenotypes are 114 morphologically indistinguishable from *sta6*.

115

116 **Culture conditions in analyzed RNA-Seq datasets** The current RNA-Seq study of 117 *cw15* and *sta6*, designated WUSTL (17 samples per strain), employed cultures grown to 4 x 10<sup>6</sup> 118 cells/ml in phosphate-buffered HSM (61) medium containing 20 mM potassium acetate at 25 μE m-2 s-1 119 light intensity. Cells were harvested by centrifugation (1153 x g 120 for 3 min) and resuspended in HSM+acetate lacking ammonium (nitrogen-free; NF). For 121 acetate boost, an appropriate aliquot of a 1.5 M potassium acetate stock was added to a 122 culture to augment its acetate concentration by an additional 20 mM.

123

124 The two sta6/*cw15* datasets from the Merchant/Pellegrini laboratories at the University of 125 California Los Angeles (6), designated UCLA1 (8 samples per strain) and UCLA2 (3 samples per strain), employed cultures grown to 4 x 10<sup>6</sup> 126 cells/ml in Tris-buffered TAP medium (20) containing 17 mM acetate at 95 μE m-2 s-1 127 light intensity, harvested by 128 centrifugation (1006 x g for 5 min), and washed once in TAP NF before resuspension in

154

155 **Viability analyses.** Two methods were used. 1) Plating efficiency. A log-phase culture 156 was resuspended in N-free HSM+acetate; after 2 days, the culture was divided and half 157 was acetate-boosted. At each time point, cells were counted, subjected to serial dilutions 158 in TAP medium, mixed with top agar, overlain on 1.5% TAP-agar plates, and allowed to 159 grow until colonies were visible. Plates with scorable colony numbers (50-150) were 160 recorded and colony number/number of cells plated (plating efficiency) was calculated. 161 The plating efficiency for log-phase cells was set as 100%, and N-starved values, with or 162 without boost, were expressed proportionately. 2) Evans Blue exclusion. A log-phase 163 culture was resuspended in N-free HSM+acetate; after 2 days, the culture was divided 164 and half was acetate-boosted. At each time point, cells were counted, mixed 1:1 with a 165 0.1% aqueous solution of Evans Blue, and scored by light microscopy as viable if dye 166 was excluded. % viability = (viable cell count/original cell count) x 100.

167

168 **Acetate uptake.** This experiment was performed 3 times with equivalent results; data from one experiment are shown. Vegetative *sta6* cells were grown to 4 x 10<sup>6</sup> 169 cells/ml in 170 HSM+acetate, pelleted, and resuspended at the same density in N-free HSM+acetate. 171 At the time points indicated in Fig. 4, 7-ml samples were centrifuged at 10,000 x g for 5 172 min; the supernatant was collected by aspiration, passed through a 0.22 μm filter, snap-173 frozen in liquid N2, and stored at -20°C until analysis. The filtered media samples were 174 diluted 50% (v/v) with D2O containing a known amount of alanine, which served as an 175 internal standard. Proton NMR for these samples were collected on a 14.09-tesla NMR spectrometer (600Mhz <sup>1</sup> 176 H resonance) using water suppression pulse sequence to suppress the <sup>1</sup> 177 H peak due to water in the media. Each spectrum was collected for 4 178 scans with a recycle delay of 10 sec. The CH3 protons of alanine are visible at 1.5ppm 179 and the CH3 protons of acetate are visible at 1.9ppm. The integrated proton peak 180 intensities are directly proportional to the molar ratios of those protons. Hence the

182 integrals of the CH3 protons of acetate to the CH3 protons of alanine of known

183 concentration.

184

185 **RNA-seq analysis.** For RNA extraction, cell density was determined with a

186 hemacytomer at each time point. 20 ml of culture was transferred at indicated time

points (maximum cell number per reaction = 2 x 10<sup>8</sup> 187 ) to a 50 ml Falcon tube and

188 centrifuged at 2000 x g for 5 min at room temperature. The supernatant was immediately

189 decanted and the pellets snap-frozen in liquid N2 and stored at -80°C.

Protein sequences were aligned using MAFFT aligner, 231 followed by manual refinement. 232

233 The *GPD* phylogeny (Fig. S2) is a neighbor-joining tree with bootstrap values from 500 234 replicates. *GPD* homologs were collected from gene models for *C. reinhardtii* (V10.2), *V.*  235 *carteri* (V1.0), and *Arabidopsis thaliana* (V10). Protein sequences were aligned using 236 MAFFT aligner, followed by manual refinement. Homology domain information was 237 obtained at the Pfam site (http://pfam.sanger.ac.uk).

238

239 The *FBA* phylogeny (Fig. S3) is a Bayesian consensus tree with bootstrap values from 240 1000 iterative samplings using MrBayes (28). *FBA* homologs were collected from gene 241 models for *C. reinhardtii* (V10.2), *V*. *carteri* (V1.0), and *A. thaliana* (V10). Protein 242 sequences were aligned using MAFFT aligner, followed by manual refinement. The 243 *FBA4* gene is truncated (apparently not due to a gene model error), deleting 100 amino 244 acids at the C-terminus, but retains homology to a full-length *V*. *carteri* member, forming 245 a divergent clade. The topology of the tree was modified to generate coherent family 246 groupings.

247

248 The GFY phylogenies (Figs. S5A, S5C, and S5D) were constructed as follows. Multiple 249 sequence alignments were performed using MUSCLE (14) in MEGA 5.2.2 (63). | 282 | Acquisition of obesity by sta6                                                          |
| 283 |                                                                                         |

284 Figure 1 shows *sta6* cultures that were acetate-boosted 2 days after N-starvation (48 h 285 NF) and centrifuged (10,000 x g for 5 min) 2, 3, 4, 5, and 8 days after the boost. Insets 286 show cells at 2 and 8 days post-boost.

287

288 Three features are evident. 1) As documented in Goodson *et al.* (19), the LBs greatly 289 increase in size. 2) The cells progressively degrade their chlorophyll and become bright 290 yellow, perhaps reflecting the increase in carotenoid content reported for N-starved *C.*  291 *reinhardtii* (9). 3) The cells become sufficiently TAG-filled that they float, even when 292 centrifuged, the hallmark feature of obesity. Subsequently, the boosted cells die, turn 293 white, and lyse; the released LBs float along with the cells, while the white cellular debris 294 pellets (Fig. 1 asterisk).

295

#### 296 **Viability of boosted vs. non-boosted cells**

297

298 Fig. 2 compares living boosted and non-boosted *sta6* cells using phase microscopy. As 299 previously noted (19), the non-boosted cells display large vacuoles by three days in N-300 free media, which we interpreted as an indication of morbidity. However, as ascertained 301 by two different assays (Table S1) viability is not compromised until six days N-free, after 302 which it slowly declines. Boosted cells display similar viability profiles (Table S1) but they 303 do not develop large vacuoles and contain more abundant LBs.

304

305 Quick-freeze deep-etch EM images of the vacuoles in 96 h NF non-boosted cells are

306 shown in Fig. 3. Contents include profiles of membrane whorls ("myelin figures"), a

307 hallmark of autophagy. No such autophagosomes are encountered in boosted cells.

308 These observations indicate that the boost somehow averts the initiation of an

309 autophagocytic response at 48 h NF, a response that is accompanied by diminished

310 TAG accumulation.

311

## 312 **Rates of acetate uptake**

313

314 An obvious explanation for the boost's ability to enhance TAG content is that after 048 315 h NF, acetate levels in the medium are exhausted and restored by the boost.

316 Alternatively, the boost might enhance the rate of acetate uptake. Either scenario would

317 provide the cells with more substrate for TAG synthesis.

318

| 319 | To test these possibilities, NMR was used to determine acetate levels in the culture      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 320 | medium. As previously reported (71), log-phase sta6 cells take up acetate very rapidly    |
| 321 | such that it is exhausted within 48 h growth, whereas non-growing N-starved cells utilize |
| 322 | it much more slowly. Our results (Fig. 4) confirm these observations: more than half the  |
| 323 | media acetate is still remaining after 048 h NF, with the mean rate of depletion (193    |
| 324 | μmol/h) similar to the rate observed by Blaby et al. (6) (166 μmol/h). The rate of        |
| 325 | depletion following acetate boost (157 μmol/h) is, if anything, slower than before boost, |
| 326 | and samples taken at short intervals following the boost (Fig. 4 inset) show no spike in  |
| 327 | acetate depletion rates with acetate addition. Hence neither hypothesis is supported,     |
| 328 | albeit a small transient influx is not likely to be detectable by these measurements.     |
| 329 |                                                                                           |
| 330 | Evidence for a transient acetate influx has instead come from RNA-Seq analysis of         |
| 331 | boosted cw15 cells. As documented in Supplementary Dataset 1B and summarized in           |
| 332 | Table 1, an increase in expression of 229 flagellum-related genes is observed within the  |
| 333 | first 2 h after boost. It has been known for some time that when the pH of the medium is  |
|     |                                                                                           |

334 dropped to pH 4.5 with 0.5 N acetic acid for 1 min and then neutralized, *C. reinhardtii*

335 cells first deflagellate and then up-regulate expression of their flagellum-related genes 336 and construct new flagella; a recent RNA-Seq profile of these genes (2) strongly 337 overlaps the genes listed in Supplementary Dataset 1B. Although *cw15* and *sta6* are 338 flagella-less, Cheshire *et al.* (12) document that such gene up-regulation also occurs in 339 "bald" strains. Deflagellation is induced by several organic acids but not by inorganic 340 acids (25), indicating that entry of organic acids, and not external pH, is the causative 341 event. Even at near-neutral pH, increasing the concentration of exogenous acetate 342 stimulates the % deflagellation response (25).

- 343
- 344 Intriguingly, only 5 flagella-related genes are up-regulated with boost in the *sta6* strain
- 345 (Table 1 and Supplementary Dataset 3B). Acetate (pKa = 4.76) is known to cross cell
- 346 membranes in its protonated state and then release the proton into the cytoplasm (4, 17).

347 Therefore, either the *sta6* cytoplasm is at a higher resting pH and/or better buffered than

348 *cw15*, or else *sta6* is for some reason not responsive to some feature of the

349 deflagellation/reflagellation signal, hypotheses we plan to test. Meanwhile, the boost-

350 induced changes in expression of non-flagellar genes, described in the next sections,

351 are apparently elicited by stimuli that can act independently of the pathway that elicits

352 the flagellar-gene response since the non-flagellar responses occur equivalently in both

353 the *cw15* and the *sta6* strains (Table 1).

354

### 355 **RNA-Seq experiments: General considerations**

356

357 Another way that the boost might enhance TAG levels is by influencing gene expression

- 358 such that, for example, enzymes involved in TAG biosynthesis and/or polar-lipid re-
- 359 cycling are more abundant. To test this thesis, RNA was sampled from *cw15* and *sta6*

360 cultures at nine intervals during 048 h NF and at eight intervals during the two days

```
361 following acetate boost at 48 h NF, and subjected to RNA-Seq analysis.
```

362

363 We first identified genes whose expression levels increase or decrease ≥2-fold within the 364 2 h following acetate boost compared with levels at 48 h NF, where in most cases the 365 boost effect subsided several hours later. Supplementary Datasets 1-4 provide complete 366 lists of these genes and their RPKM (Reads Per Kilobase of exon model per Million 367 mapped reads) values where, notably, some half of the genes are not annotated so we 368 may well be missing some important or even key participants. Table 1 summarizes the 369 outcome: Of the estimated 17,301 genes in v.4 of the *C. reinhardtii* genome, 875 non-370 flagellar genes displayed a ≥2-fold up-shift in *sta6* and 870 in *cw15* with acetate boost, 371 while 429 genes display a ≥2-fold down-shift in *sta6* and 428 in *cw15*. These numbers 372 might suggest that the same gene sets are responsive in both strains, but this is not the 373 case: only 34% of the up-regulated genes are shared and only 43% of the down-374 regulated genes are shared (identified in Supplementary Datasets 1-4, last column). 375 376 We then focused on those genes that encode participants in biosynthetic and metabolic 377 pathways related to C- and N-flux and stress-related processes. The genes considered 378 in this report are highlighted in Supplementary Datasets 1-4. We also queried boost-non-379 responsive genes that participate in the same pathways as boost-responsive genes. 380 381 In analyzing these data, designated WUSTL, we ran comparative studies with the RNA-382 Seq data on 048 h NF in *cw15* and *sta6*, designated UCLA1 (8 samples per strain) 383 and, in some cases, UCLA2 (3 samples per strain) (6), derived from cells cultured under 384 different conditions than the WUSTL conditions. On occasion, we also included data

385 from N-starved wild-type cells maintained in the presence (UCLA-WT) (8) or absence

386 (UTSW-WT) (48) of acetate. Details of strains and culture conditions are provided in

387 Methods.

388

| 389 | Comparison of expression patterns documented that most of genes involved in the           |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 390 | assessed metabolic and biosynthetic pathways were expressed concordantly in the           |
| 391 | WUSTL and UCLA experiments—e.g., "holding steady," or "increasing/decreasing," or         |
| 392 | "rising and then falling"—even though culture conditions and input transcript levels were |
| 393 | disparate, establishing these genes' response to N-depletion as "robust" to               |
| 394 | environmental influence. This steady baseline allowed recognition of the few genes        |
| 395 | whose expression patterns were not concordant between experiments, where in many          |
| 396 | cases these genes also proved to be responsive to acetate boost.                          |
| 397 |                                                                                           |
| 398 | Absent from our data are no-boost controls, and such information would be of particular   |
| 399 | value with respect to the autophagy-related genes described in a later section. In        |
| 400 | general, however, the thousands of genes in the dataset that do not respond to boost      |
| 401 | continue to follow their 048 h NF trajectory during the ensuing 4896 h NF interval,     |
| 402 | most either holding steady or drifting downward. There is every reason to assume,         |
| 403 | therefore, that this would also be the case for the boost-responsive genes had a boost    |
| 404 | not been administered.                                                                    |
| 405 |                                                                                           |
| 406 | Genes related to carbon flux with "robust" expression patterns                            |
| 407 |                                                                                           |
| 408 | Starch, fatty-acid, and TAG biosynthesis. Expression levels for genes involved with       |
| 409 | starch, fatty-acid, and TAG biosynthesis are shown in Tables 2-4.                         |
|     |                                                                                           |

410

| 411 | As initially noted by Blaby et al. (6) and confirmed here, the starch-related genes are       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 412 | uniformly up-regulated soon after cells are subjected to N-depletion (Table 2, yellow         |
| 413 | highlight), while genes encoding enzymes involved in fatty-acid biosynthesis are up           |
| 414 | regulated only at 24 h NF (Table 3, yellow highlight), patterns that mirror the observed      |
| 415 | early increase in starch formation and the later increase in TAG (59). Genes encoding         |
| 416 | TAG-related enzymes show two patterns: some are up-regulated early and then decline,          |
| 417 | while others show a steady increase, with maximal values at 48 h NF that persist out to       |
| 418 | 96 h NF (Table 4, yellow highlight). The strong concordance of these patterns between         |
| 419 | the WUSTL and UCLA datasets indicates that these are "robust" genetic programs, akin          |
| 420 | to those governing expression of chloroplast ribosomal proteins (Table S2), persisting        |
| 421 | despite differences in strains and culture conditions and despite the fact that sta6 fails to |
| 422 | synthesize any starch and produces chloroplast LBs.                                           |

424 Starch-related genes are generally insensitive to boost, the three exceptions highlighted 425 in orange in Table 2. Most show a transient increase in expression at 24 h NF in the 426 WUSTL-sta6 samples (Table 2, red highlight).

427

428 Fatty acid synthesis-related genes are down-regulated at 2 h NF and start recovering at

429 12 h NF, with maximal expression at 24 h NF continuing out to 96 h NF (Table 3).

430 Transcript levels are consistently higher in *sta6* than *cw15* at 48 h NF and, in the

431 WUSTL data, consistently higher in *cw15* by 96 h NF. While transcription of these genes

432 is not enhanced by boost, it is briefly depressed in several cases, usually in *sta6* (Table

433 3, purple highlight). Notably, the five genes encoding subunits of the multimeric

434 "prokaryotic" acetyl-CoA carboxylase (ACCase)—*ACX1*, *BCX1*, *BCR1*, *BXP1*, and

435 *BXP2*—are robustly expressed in both strains (Table 3), whereas the gene

436 Cre08.g373050 encoding a monomeric "eukaryotic" ACCase produces few (0-6 RPKMs) 437 transcripts in both strains (Table 3).

438

439 DAG TAG conversion is canonically catalyzed by type-1 (DGAT) and type-2 (DGTT) 440 diacylglycerol acyltransferases. Confirming four reports (6, 8, 45, 47), expression of 441 *DGTT1* is strongly up-regulated during 048 h NF, with further increases during the 442 next two days without a boost response (Table 4), establishing this enzyme as a robust 443 player in the TAG-biosynthesis response in all tested strains. Confirming Blaby *et al.* (6), 444 expression of *DGTT2* in *sta6* is initially 3- to 6-fold higher than in *cw15*; by 8 h NF it 445 tapers to *cw15* levels, but following boost, levels are 2-fold higher in *sta6* out to 96 h NF 446 (Table 4 blue highlight). This distinctive *DGTT2* transcription pattern is the first of four 447 *sta6*/*cw15* differences that will be lifted up in this report. *DGTT4* and *DGAT1* peak early 448 and their enhanced levels are sustained to 96 h NF in both strains (Table 4). The 449 WUSTL and UCLA data are dissimilar in two respects: 1) *DGTT3* transcripts peak early 450 and then decline in WUSTL whereas they peak late in UCLA; and 2) Cre06.g310200 451 (DGAT3-like) peaks late in the WUSTL *sta6* sample and in the two *cw15* samples, 452 whereas it peaks early in the UCLA *sta6* sample. 453 454 TAG synthesis can also be catalyzed by phospholipid diacylglycerol acyltransferase 455 (PDAT), which uses fatty acids from polar lipids to acylate the DAG hydroxyl group. 456 Recent studies variously report that PDAT makes a 25% contribution (8) or only a minor

457 contribution (72) to TAG synthesis under N-deprivation conditions. Transcription from

458 *PDAT1* peaks early and remains steady in all three strains, with a transient down-

459 regulation at boost (Table 4).

460

461 Two other lipid-related genes augmented with boost in both strains are Cre06.g299800 462 (long-chain-fatty-acid CoA ligase) and *FAD3* (fatty acid desaturase) (Table 4). A putative 463 long-chain acyl-CoA synthetase (*LCS1*) decreases with boost while a second (*LCS2*) is 464 not affected (Table 4).

465

466 The *MLDP1* gene increases expression 28-fold in *cw15* and 21-fold in *sta6* during 048 467 h NF and sustains high levels for the next two days without a boost response; similar 468 increases are seen in the UCLA and UCLA-WT data (Table 4). The gene product, 469 originally posited to be associated with lipid bodies (major lipid droplet protein) (46), has 470 recently been shown to instead be associated with ER membranes (27), where it 471 possibly participates in the intimate ER/LB associations that are established during 472 cytoplasmic LB formation in N-free media (19). 473 474 **Lipases.** In their pioneering RNA-Seq study, Miller *et al.* (45) identified 130 *C. reinhardtii* 475 genes carrying the GXSXG motif expected of lipases, of which 46 were either up-476 regulated (75%) or down-regulated (25%) ≥2-fold in wild-type cells during 048 h NF, 477 and they posited that a subset of these might be involved in releasing fatty acids from 478 polar lipids for use in TAG formation in the fashion of the PDAT enzyme. In a 479 subsequent paper (39), the lab reported that one of these genes, Cre03.g193500 and 480 now named *PGD1* (plastid galactoglycerolipid degradation), indeed contributes to TAG 481 formation by breaking down pre-existing chloroplast monogalactosyldiacylglycerol 482 (MGDG) into its lyso-lipid form for re-acylation as TAG; knockdown of this gene results in 483 reduced TAG accumulation with N-starvation. 484 485 The WUSTL RNA-Seq data for these 46 candidate lipase genes is shown in Table S3; 486 four genes annotated as "TAG lipases" and not included in the 46-gene set are also

| 490 | expression, with some showing small boost responses. The three genes in blue font in          |
| 491 | Table S3 are displayed in Table 5 and given additional attention below.                       |

513 predicted signal peptide, which usually indicates an ER secretory destination but in 514 some cases directs proteins to the chloroplast (36, 67). The gene is not expressed in 515 UCLA-WT nor in *cw15* except a brief spike at boost, but is robustly expressed by *sta6* in 516 both the WUSTL and UCLA1/UCLA2 experiments (Table 5, blue highlight), with a 2.5- 517 fold increase with boost (Table 5 orange highlight). Cre17.g735600 is related to three 518 other *C. reinhardtii* genes (data not shown): Cre09.g399400 and Cre14.g615550 yield no 519 transcripts in either strain, while Cre02.g127300 shows a steady increase in both (Table 520 S3). The distinctive transcription pattern of Cre17.g735600 is the third of four *sta6*/*cw15*  521 differences that will be lifted up in this report.

522

523 Cre02.g127550 (g9712 in the v.5.3 assembly), which lacks a predicted leader sequence, 524 displays strong expression in *sta6* but weak expression in *cw15*, except for a brief spike 525 at boost, throughout the 96 h time course in the WUSTL experiment (Table 5). However, 526 expression of this gene is anomalous. In the two UCLA experiments, transcripts are 527 either somewhat more abundant in *cw15* than *sta6* (UCLA1) or equivalent (UCLA2); 528 moreover, the gene shows no expression in UCLA-WT (Table 5) whereas it is the most 529 strongly up-regulated candidate lipase (5.6-fold) in the Miller *et al.* (45) wild-type 530 experiment. This example lifts up the value of having several data sets when assigning 531 expression patterns to particular strains: such anomalies, and this one is obviously of 532 interest, presumably indicate sensitivity to particular culture conditions rather than a 533 strain-specific trait.

534

535 **Fatty-acid β-oxidation enzymes.** TAG accumulation operates in opposition to TAG 536 breakdown. The fatty acids released from the breakdown of both neutral and polar lipids 537 are processed by the β-oxidation pathway. In their RNA-Seq comparison of wild-type 538 cells at 0 h and 48 h NF, Miller *et al.* (45) note >3-fold decreased transcript levels for two

539 enzymes in this pathway, acyl-CoA oxidase (Cre16.g689050) and 3-oxoacyl-CoA 540 thiolase (Cre17.g723650, *ATO1*), and a 2-fold increase in a third, enoyl-CoA hydratase 541 (Cre03.g190850, *ECH1*). Similar patterns are seen in the present experiments (Table 542 S4): expression levels of the oxidase gene decline slightly and the thiolase gene more 543 substantially, while the hydratase levels increase. Interestingly, levels of thiolase and 544 hydratase transcripts increase 3.9-fold and 1.6-fold respectively with acetate boost for 545 *sta6*, with no boost effect for *cw15*, and thiolase gene expression remains stronger in 546 *sta6* out to 96 h NF (Table S4). Therefore, the boost appears to selectively enhance the 547 machinery for fatty-acid breakdown in *sta6*, perhaps in part in conjunction with the 548 breakdown and remodeling of thylakoid membranes undertaken by *sta6* at later time 549 points in the N-starvation sequence (19).

550

551 **Glycerol-3-P dehydrogenase.** Glycerol-3-P serves as the backbone for DAG and TAG 552 biosynthesis and can thus be said to serve as a bridge between carbohydrate and lipid 553 biosynthesis. It also drives a mitochondrial shuttle system engaged in the import of 554 NADH (34). Glycerol-3-P is generated from dihydroxyacetone phosphate (DHAP), a 555 sugar produced during both gluconeogenesis and the Calvin-Benson cycle, via NADH-556 dependent glycerol-3-P dehydrogenases (GPDH) (the mitochondrial enzymes are 557 FADH2-dependent). NADH-dependent GPDHs are encoded by five genes in *C.*  558 *reinhardtii* (Table 6), one of which (*GPD5*) was identified during this study. 559 560 Table 6 summarizes their expression patterns. *GPD1* (Cre12.g511150) and *GPD5* 561 (Cre02.g122300/g9595), with no predicted targeting sequences, are expressed 562 constitutively at low levels throughout the time course. *GPD2* (Cre01.g053000) 563 transcripts, predicted to be chloroplast-targeted, increase 26-fold in *cw15* and 24-fold in

564 *sta6* during 048 h NF; boost increases transcript levels another 2-fold in *cw15* and 2.3-

565 fold in *sta6*, and high levels are sustained to 96 h NF. *GPD4* (Cre10.g421700), also 566 predicted to be chloroplast-directed, increases expression during 048 h NF (4.3-fold 567 *cw15*, 6.2-fold *sta6*) and sustains high levels to 96 h NF without responding to boost, 568 with levels consistently higher in *sta6* than in *cw15*. Large increases in *GPD2* and *GPD4* 569 transcripts during 048 h NF, and the *sta6* bias for *GPD4*, are also seen in the UCLA 570 and UCLA-WT data (Table 6).

571

572 Particularly striking is the pattern of *GPD3* (Cre01.g053150)*,* which carries no predicted 573 leader sequence. Its expression level remains very low throughout the 048 h NF 574 period in both strains in both experiments, but then shoots up 30-fold with boost in *cw15* 575 and 72-fold in *sta6* (Table 6). Moreover, while transcript levels then abate in *cw15* to 576 39% of their maximum boost levels by 96 h NF, they continue to strongly increase in 577 *sta6*, reaching 217% of their maximum boost levels at 96 h NF (Table 6, blue highlight), 578 such that *GPD3* expression levels at 96 h NF are 12-fold higher in *sta6* than in *cw15*. 579 The distinctive *GPD3* transcription pattern is the fourth of four *sta6*/*cw15* differences that 580 will be lifted up in this report.

581

582 The five *GDP* genes are members of three subfamilies (Fig. S2). The genes in the third 583 subfamily (*GDP2*-*GDP4*) have an additional feature: they carry an N-terminal HAD 584 domain encoding a hydrolase sequence with homology to the enzyme 3-phosphoserine 585 phosphatase (PSP) which catalyzes the final and irreversible step of serine biosynthesis 586 (32). The *Arabidopsis* member of the subfamily lacks this domain but it is present in the 587 *V. carteri* member. An independent *PSP1* gene in *C. reinhardtii* maintains steady 588 expression throughout 096 h NF (not shown). Assuming that the PSP domains of the 589 GPDH proteins are operant, then two enzymatic activities would be stimulated with up-590 expression of *GPD2*-*GPD4*.

| 592 | TCA and glyoxylate cycles. Genes encoding enzymes proposed (8) as components of     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 593 | the TCA cycle show either steady or slowly declining 048 h NF expression in both   |
| 594 | strains, with concordant WUSTL and UCLA trajectories (Table S5A). Only three genes  |
| 595 | show up-regulation with boost (Table S5A, orange highlight). The CIS2 gene is given |
| 596 | further consideration in a later section.                                           |

597

598 The less familiar glyoxylate cycle, which is absent in animals, shares many enzymes 599 with the TCA cycle and permits the net synthesis of a 4-C product (succinate) from two 600 acetyl units; the succinate is then metabolized to malate/oxaloacetate, which feed into 601 gluconeogenesis. Table S5B shows the three genes encoding enzymes proposed (8) as 602 components of the glyoxylate cycle that show robust expression, where only *MDH2* 603 shows a modest increase in *sta6* with acetate boost. Two other genes unique to the 604 glyoxylate cycle, *ICL1* and *MAS1*, show a sensitive expression pattern and a strong 605 stimulation with boost, and are considered in a later section.

606

607 **Acetyl-CoA.** Acetate enters both the TCA and glyoxylate cycles as acetyl-CoA, and 608 acetyl-CoA is also the acetate donor for fatty-acid biosynthesis. Acetyl-CoA synthetases 609 are encoded by three genes. *ACS1* expression, while steady, is far lower in *sta6* than 610 *cw15* in the WUSTL data but equivalent in the UCLA data (Table S5C), again lifting up 611 the value of having multiple data sets when attempting to discern strain-specific 612 expression patterns. *ACS2* and *ACS3* both increase expression with N-deprivation in a 613 sensitive fashion and are considered in a later section. 614 615 A second avenue to acetyl-CoA synthesis is catalyzed by multimeric pyruvate

616 dehydrogenases that convert pyruvate to acetyl-CoA and CO2. The expression patterns

617 of two genes, *PDH2* (mitochondrial subunit) and *PDC2* (chloroplast subunit) (Table S5C), 618 exemplify those encoding the other subunits: transcription is strong and steady out to 96 619 h NF without a boost effect.

620

621 A third avenue, the cleavage of citrate via ATP citrate lyase, is important in the

622 generation of acetyl-CoA in the oleaginous yeast *Yarrowia lipolytica* (5), but *ACLA1* and

623 *ACLB1* transcripts are steady during 096 h NF and *ACLA1* expression decreases with 624 boost (Table S5C).

625

626 Acetyl-CoA can also be generated via acetate kinase (*ACK1*, *ACK2*) and

627 acetylphosphotransferase (*PAT1*, *PAT2*), and while *PAT1* levels are steady, the other

628 three decline >2-fold in expression with boost (Table S5C), suggesting that this pathway 629 is not a major participant.

630

631 **Gluconeogenesis/glycolysis and the Calvin-Benson cycle.** Gluconeogenesis and the 632 Calvin Benson cycle both engage in generating hexose phosphates that have two 633 alternative anabolic destinations: feeding into the oxidative pentose phosphate pathway 634 with the concomitant generation of NADPH, or feeding into starch biosynthesis. They 635 can also be catabolized via glycolysis. In a recent review, Johnson and Alric (31) note 636 that enzymes mediating the "upper half" of gluconeogenesis (from 3-phosphoglycerate 637 to hexose phosphates) appear to be plastid-localized in *C. reinhardtii*, where their 638 activities may overlap with Calvin-Benson-cycle enzyme activities. 639 640 The robust members of the gluconeogenesis pathway and Calvin-Benson cycle are 641 listed in Tables S5D and S5E respectively; sensitive members are considered in a

642 subsequent section. An obvious anomaly is the high initial level of expression of the

695 Abrupt N-starvation of log-phase cells is by definition stressful. An early response is the 696 stimulated expression of members of the Target of Rapamycin (TOR)-related autophagy 697 pathway (50). Of the seven annotated *APG* genes (called *ATG* in most organisms) that 698 respond to *TOR* signaling in other organisms, all are strongly and coordinately up-699 regulated starting at 2 h NF, and all except *APG10* remain elevated for the next 96 h, 700 with only *APG4* showing a positive boost response and several a modest negative 701 response (Table S7A). 702 703 Expression of most *PEX*, *PRX*, and *MSD* genes that participate in ROS scavenging 704 remains steady or decreases in both experiments throughout the time course (not 705 shown), perhaps because chlorophyll levels (8, 9, 47, 59, 71) and photosynthetic

706 electron transport activity (37, 71) are decreasing and such toxic products are not a

707 major issue. Table S7B shows the four genes in this category with a modest boost

708 response. None of the genes encoding *SRR* scavenger receptors responds to boost;

709 *SRR16* shows stronger expression in *sta6* than *cw15* in the WUSTL but not the UCLA

710 data (Table S7B), yet another example of the value of having 2 data sets.

711 Table S7C includes several stress-related low-CO2-inducible (*LCI*) genes that are boost-

712 upregulated and a high-CO2-inducible (*FEA1*) gene to illustrate that members of this

713 cohort are highly variable in expression patterns both between strains and between

714 experiments.

715

## 716 **Respiratory burst oxidase**

717

718 The gene *RBO1*, encoding a homologue of the respiratory burst oxidase that responds 719 to stress in land plants (42, 62), is contiguous to the *STA6* gene and deleted in the *sta6*

720 mutant (6), suggesting that its absence might influence the *sta6* phenotype (6). As

721 shown in Table S7D, *RBO1* produces few transcripts in *cw15* and UCLA-WT during

722 048 h NF, and is unresponsive to boost.

723

- 724 A full copy of the contiguous orthologue *RBO2* is present in *sta6*, but it yields few
- 725 transcripts (Table S7D), suggesting that the deletion of *RBO1* curtails *RBO2* expression
- 726 as well (6). *RBO2* expression is also low in wild type; in *cw15*, it decreases in the UCLA
- 727 data and increases in the WUSTL data, where it stabilizes without boost (Table S7D).

728

- 729 Blaby *et al.* (6) note that in the UCLA experiments, expression of *LHCSR1* and *LHCSR2*,
- 730 both involved in photoquenching, is higher in *cw15* than in *sta6* (Table S7D), and
- 731 suggest that *RBO1*/*RBO2* might play a role in their induction. However, in the WUSTL
- 732 data, both genes are more strongly transcribed in *sta6* than in *cw15* (Table S7D).

733

- 734 A key test of this hypothesis—the effect of an *RBO1* transgene on the *sta6* phenotype—
- 735 is currently in progress in the UCLA labs.
- 736

### 737 **Carbon-related genes with "sensitive" expression patterns**

738

- 739 **The blue/green cohort.** Five boost-enhanced genes—*ICL1*, *MAS1*, *PCK1*, *TAL1*, and
- 740 *FBP1—*were of immediate interest because these genes were identified in the UCLA
- 741 study (6) as having patterns of expression that were similar within a strain but markedly
- 742 different when *cw15* and *sta6* were compared. Specifically, expression of the five genes
- 743 sank or stayed low during 048 h NF in *cw15*, but increased, after an initial drop, in *sta6*
- 744 (6).

746 When we analyzed the 048 h NF expression patterns of these five genes in the 747 WUSTL data, they also proved to be similar within a strain and distinctive between 748 strains. However, the patterns observed were quite different from those observed in the 749 UCLA study. As shown in Fig. 5, transcription of these genes in *cw15* is high through 12 750 h NF, drops at 24 h NF, and is <40% of starting values by 48 h NF (blue bars), while 751 transcription in *sta6* generally stays low throughout the time course except for a strong 752 spike at 24 h NF (green bar), when the transcripts are >50% more abundant than at 48 h 753 NF. The RPKM values for these gene sets are found in Table 7.

754

755 We went on to identify 16 additional genes that display the blue/green pattern in the 756 WUSTL dataset; all but *ACS2*, *FBA3*, *PGK1*, *PRK1* and *RPE1* show the late <40% blue 757 drop in expression in *cw15* compared with starting values, and all but *CIS2* show the 758 >50% green spike in expression in *sta6* at 24 h NF compared with 48 h NF values (Fig. 5 759 and Table 7). Fig. S4 shows WUSTL expression profiles for other genes whose products 760 function in the same pathways as the genes shown in Table 7, and none displays the 761 blue/green pattern. Most of the 21 genes responded to acetate boost to at least some 762 extent (Fig. 5); those with a ≥2-fold increase are highlighted in orange in Table 7.

763

764 None of the additional 16 genes displays the blue/green pattern in the UCLA1 data, and 765 only *ACS3*, GFY3, and Cre15.g641200 displays the *cw15* down/*sta6* up pattern of the 766 five founder UCLA genes (Table 7). Instead, in both UCLA experiments, their expression 767 tends to decrease gradually in both strains (Table 7 and Table S8). Table S8 also 768 displays data from the UCLA-WT experiment where expression patterns are again 769 different, with transcript levels generally higher than in UCLA-cw.

770

| 771 | Unlike the genes grouped together in previous sections, the 21 genes listed in Table 7     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 772 | encode proteins that operate in a number of different pathways. Isocitrate lyase (ICL1)    |

| 781 | Benson cycle; Cre15.g641200 is annotated as a candidate mitochondrial fatty acid           |
| 782 | carrier albeit it lacks a mitochondrial targeting sequence; and aspartate                  |

| 784 | to glutamate and oxaloacetate, which feeds into the pentose phosphate pathway. The         |

|     |                                                                                            |

| 787 | (GAP3) are predicted to be members of the Calvin-Benson cycle (6). It should be noted,     |

| 790 | segregated. An anomaly related to CIS2 is considered in the following section.             |
| 791 |                                                                                            |
| 792 | Taken together, it appears that a group of 21 genes, whose products function in various    |
| 793 | pathways, respond to 048 h NF and acetate boost as a cohort in the WUSTL                  |
| 794 | experiment, whereas their expression patterns differ in both the UCLA and UCLA-WT          |
| 795 | experiments. We suggest that these differences relate to the fact that the three           |

797 laboratory conditions (*e.g.*, media and light intensity; see Methods), and propose that the 798 listed genes are singularly sensitive to the cell's metabolic status, perhaps because their 799 products serve as "gateway" members of their respective pathways. They might, for 800 example, have short half-lives and/or govern rate-limiting reactions and hence serve as 801 nodes that permit gene-expression levels to influence the course of metabolism or 802 biosynthesis. We therefore refer to these as "sensitive genes," as contrasted with the 803 "robust genes" whose expression patterns are concordant, with minor variations, within 804 the three experiments.

805

806 Support for this proposal comes from the recent study of an *ICL1* deletion mutant (51), 807 devoid of a glyoxylate cycle, which displays many anomalies in central carbon metabolism. 14N/15 808 N labeling experiments show that many of the proteins designated 809 here as sensitive—specifically, those encoded by *MAS1*, *CIS2*, *FBA1*, *PCK1*, *ACS3*, 810 *TAL1*, and *AST1*—are either strongly increased or decreased in the mutant relative to 811 controls. 812 **Genes whose expression is stimulated by acetate.** Thirteen of the 21 sensitive genes

813 in Table 7 elevate expression ≥2-fold with acetate boost (orange highlights) whereas the 814 others show a weaker or no response. Expression of the strongly boost-responsive set is 815 also highly sensitive to the presence of exogenous acetate in N-replete media. Table 8 816 compares their transcript levels during N-replete log-phase growth in acetate-containing 817 media (WUSTL) versus acetate-free media (UTSW) (48). The genes that show ≥2-fold 818 responses to acetate boost prove to be expressed at very low levels when growing on 819 acetate-free media (Table 8A, columns 3 and 4) compared with acetate-containing 820 media (Table 8A, columns 1, 2 and 7), with low expression persisting after 18 h NF in 821 acetate-free media (Table 8A, columns 5 and 6). By contrast, sensitive genes showing 822 modest or no boost responses are expressed equivalently (Table 8B) or at higher levels

823 (Table 8C) when growing on acetate-free media compared with acetate-containing 824 media, with expression usually declining after 18 h NF in acetate-free media (Table 8B 825 and C, columns 5 and 6). Hence sensitive genes displaying a strong acetate boost prove 826 to also display a strong transcriptional sensitivity to the presence/absence of exogenous 827 acetate. Notably, 7 of these 13 proteins, listed in the previous paragraph, are also 828 expressed aberrantly in the *ICL1* mutant (51).

829

830 In the course of this analysis we encountered an anomaly pertaining to the *CIS1* and 831 *CIS2* (citrate synthase) genes. The former has been predicted as encoding a glyoxylate-832 cycle enzyme and the latter as encoding a TCA-cycle enzyme (6), assignments that 833 have been adopted in Table S5A and B. However, *CIS2* shows a strong acetate-boost 834 up-regulation and low expression on acetate-free media (Table 8A), akin to the 835 dedicated *ICL1* and *MAS1* enzymes of the glyoxylate cycle (Table 8A), whereas *CIS1* is 836 boost-non-responsive (Table S5A), like other TCA-cycle genes (Table S5A), and 837 equivalently expressed in both media (Table 8B). Plancke *et al.* (51) also present 838 arguments for *CIS2* as a member of the glyoxylate cycle.

839

840 A second anomaly relates to the glyoxylate cycle itself. Both the UCLA1 and UCLA2 841 studies document increased levels of expression of *ICL1*, *MAL1*, and *CIS2* in *sta6* and 842 low expression levels in *cw15* and complemented *sta6* strains during 048 h NF, and 843 suggest that enhanced glyoxylate activity plays a role in enhanced *sta6* TAG production. 844 However, this increase is not observed in the WUSTL data. Miller *et al.* (45), who also 845 observe decreases in *ICL1* and *MAS1* expression in wild-type N-starved cells, point out 846 that depressed glyoxylate cycle activity would result in more acetate being available for 847 fatty-acid biosynthesis. The phenotype of the *ICL1* deletion mutant (50) supports their 848 proposal: the mutant generates enhanced levels of fatty acids and TAGs compared with

849 non-deleted controls even in N-replete conditions. It has also been reported that hexose 850 phosphates, which accumulate in *sta6* (6), inhibit expression of *ICL* and *MAS* genes in 851 cucumber (21). Taken together, the enhanced *sta6* glyoxylate-cycle profiles in the UCLA 852 studies during 048 h NF, confirmed with enzyme assays, presumably reflect particular 853 features of the UCLA experimental conditions, and may explain, at least in part, why 854 TAG levels in *sta6* were found to be equivalent to *cw15* at 48 h NF and not enhanced 855 until 96 h NF, whereas they are enhanced by 48 h NF under WUSTL experimental 856 conditions (19, 68).

857

858 Figure 6 shows, in blue font, the products of the 13 acetate-sensitive genes and their 859 positions in various metabolic/biosynthetic pathways. We propose that of the 21 860 blue/green genes identified in this study (Tables 7 and 8), this cluster monitors a variety 861 of activities related to acetate utilization, while the remaining 8 genes largely monitor 862 activities related to the Calvin-Benson cycle.

863

864 **Candidate acetate permeases.** The genes designated GFY3, GFY4, and GFY5 in 865 Tables 7 and 8, as well as genes GFY1 and GFY2, have been identified as members of 866 the *GPR1*/*FUN30*/*YaaH* (pfam01184) gene family (10) (Fig. S4A). Several fungal 867 proteins in this family have been shown or suggested to mediate acetate uptake (18, 49), 868 and acetate permease activity has recently been demonstrated for the *E. coli* protein 869 *YaaH* (56). In *S. cerevisiae*, both exogenous acetate and induction of the glyoxylate 870 cycle are accompanied by strong up-regulation of its *GPR1*/*FUN30*/*YaaH* gene (41, 65). 871 In *C. reinhardtii*, the five genes are closely linked on LG17, with GFY1-GFY3 in one 872 cluster and GFY4 and GFY5 apparently contiguous in a second cluster. 873

874 While GFY1 and GFY2 show constitutive expression and GFY1 transcripts actually 875 decrease with boost*,* GFY3-GFY5 show the blue/green pattern during 048 h NF (Fig. 876 5) and increase expression 3- to 13-fold with acetate boost in both strains (Fig. 5 and 877 Table 7). Expression of GFY3-GFY5 is also strong in the UCLA and UCLA-WT samples 878 (Table 7), all of which derive from cells maintained in acetate, whereas cells grown or 879 maintained in the absence of acetate have very low reads (Table 8A).

880

881 We went on to analyze this family in more detail using two approaches. Fig. S5B shows 882 a similarity network (3, 7) of 355 representative *GPR1*/*FUN30*/*YaaH* sequences (a key is 883 provided in Supplementary Dataset 6). The proteins are widely disseminated and clearly 884 separate along the eukaryote/prokaryote divide, with members absent from animals and 885 vascular land plants. The genomes of sequenced algal species encode orthologs 886 (Supplementary Dataset 6) that bear greater similarity to the prokaryotic proteins than 887 the other eukaryotic proteins (Fig. S5B), suggesting that the non-algal eukaryotes 888 (mostly fungi) and algae independently acquired the genes from prokaryotes by 889 horizontal gene transfer (HGT) in two distinct and early events. Maximum-likelihood 890 analysis (Fig. S5C) shows that the green-algal genes form a loose Clade III that is again 891 more closely related to prokaryotic (Clade I) than to eukaryotic (Clade II) family members, 892 and Fig. S5D shows that the six genes in *V*. *carteri* form a distinct subfamily from the five 893 genes in the closely related *C. reinhardtii*, lifting up the rapid evolution of these 894 sequences in algal lineages. The phylogenetic distributions of *GPR1*/*FUN30*/*YaaH* 895 genes can be highly unusual, with both moss and fungal genes in Clade II, a *Leishmania* 896 gene in the prokaryotic Clade I (presumably a recent HGT), and volvocacean genes 897 more closely related to a haptophyte gene (*E. huxleyi*) than to other chlorophyte genes 898 in Clade III.

899

946 As detailed in the Discussion, we propose that this increase/decrease pattern in 947 degradation-related genes may relate to our morphological observation (Figs. 2 and 3) 948 that acetate boost at 48 h NF appears to divert cells from pursuing an autophagocytic 949 pathway.

951

#### 952 **DISCUSSION**

953

### 954 **General overview of the TAG-accumulation response**

955

956 This study, combined with those previously published, generate the following picture of 957 the TAG-accumulation response which, while provisional and incomplete, can serve to 958 provide context for a discussion of our results.

959

960 *C. reinhardtii* cells growing in acetate-supplemented N-replete media are actively

961 running acetate-fed glyoxylate cycles and photosynthetic electron transport-fed Calvin-

962 Benson cycles. When the cells are transferred into N-free acetate media, these cycles

963 presumably continue to operate in the short term, generating glucose-6-P via both the

964 Calvin-Benson cycle and the glyoxylate cycle-derived oxaloacetate that feeds into 965 gluconeogenesis.

966

967 Early in the first day, genes encoding starch-related enzymes are up-regulated, and 968 much of the glucose-6-P is funneled into ADP-glucose and then starch biosynthesis in 969 *cw15*, but not in *sta6*. During the first day, genes encoding enzymes for glycerol-3-P, 970 fatty-acid, and TAG biosynthesis are also up-regulated in specific patterns, while genes 971 encoding enzymes for the glyoxylate cycle are in most cases down-regulated, 972 presumably allowing exogenous acetate to funnel into fatty-acid biosynthesis. TAGs 973 proceed to accumulate in cytoplasmic LBs (in both strains) and in chloroplast LBs (in 974 *sta6* but not *cw15*). 975

976 By the end of the second day, when starch levels start to plateau, (9, 16, 59), the cells

977 ordinarily shift into an autophagy program that limits the extent of TAG accumulation,

| 1056 | participate in generating the sta6 phenotype, the phenotype is also likely to be          |

| 1067 | (19).                                                                                     |
| 1068 |                                                                                           |

1069 The widely accepted model for LB formation in land plants is that fatty acids are 1070 synthesized in the chloroplast and then shuttled to the ER where they are conjugated to 1071 glycerol-3-P backbones by resident ER enzymes to generate DAGs and then TAGs. 1072 Recent studies, however, indicate that TAG biosynthesis in *C. reinhardtii* has a number 1073 of distinctive features (summarized in Fig. 9 of ref. 39): 1) the DAG moieties are largely 1074 assembled in the plastid and some are then shipped to the ER for the addition of a third 1075 acyl group (15); 2) many of the TAG fatty acids derive from pre-existing chloroplast 1076 glycerolipids that are cleaved by dedicated lipases such as PDG1 (39); and 3) the 1077 closely apposed chloroplast outer envelope membrane and ER membranes (19) are co-1078 participants in cytoplasmic LB assembly, perhaps assisted by alga-specific and ER-1079 localized MLDP proteins (27, 46). Another relevant consideration is that *C. reinhardtii* 1080 likely possesses at least one chloroplast-localized diacylglycerol acyltransferase that

1081 mediates the constitutive formation of TAG-filled plastoglobules and eyespot granules in 1082 all strains (19, 46).

1083

1084 One *sta6* scenario, then, would go as follows. 1) Some of the posited glucose-6-P 1085 backflow feeds into the plastid-localized pentose phosphate pathway to generate the 1086 NADPH required for additional fatty-acid synthesis as photosynthetic electron transfer 1087 abates. 2) Some of the backflow moves in the glycolysis direction until it forms fructose 1088 bisphosphate, some of which is then shunted into glycerol-3-P via enhanced levels of 1089 GPDH enzymes, where the sharply reduced level of fructose bisphosphate in N-starved 1090 *sta6* cells (6) is consistent with this suggestion. 3) The backflow may also inhibit 1091 operation of the glyoxylate cycle, as is reported in land plants (21), directing acetate into 1092 fatty-acid biosynthesis. 4) The augmented fatty-acid and glycerol-3-P pools, 1093 supplemented by the products of thylakoid breakdown (19), generate augmented levels 1094 of chloroplast DAG, some of which is then converted into chloroplast TAG, events 1095 mediated by the enhanced levels of DGTT2 and Cre03.g155250/ Cre17.g735600 1096 enzymes. 5) The TAG is stored in chloroplast-localized LBs, perhaps via an expansion 1097 of pre-existing plastoglobules (19). Testable features of this model include the prediction 1098 that mutations in the four *sta6*-enhanced enzymes would be compromised in chloroplast 1099 LB formation in a *sta6* background and that these enzymes are chloroplast-localized. 1100 1101 Mutations like the *sta6* deletion are expected to generate a loss-of-function phenotype, 1102 in this case an inability to form starch. Unexpected is a gain-of-function phenotype, in 1103 this case the formation of a novel class of LBs with the attendant up-regulation of at least 1104 four lipid-related genes. Chloroplast LBs have a well-defined organization and 1105 architecture (19), also unexpected for a cellular trait generated by a biochemical defect. 1106 Hence it is possible that the *sta6* genotype elicits the expression of a chloroplast-LB

1108 wild-type cells during normal laboratory conditions but is stimulated in wild-type cells 1109 under to-be-identified conditions. 1110 1111 **Acetate boost**  1112 1113 Acetate boost was discovered by accident: an additional 20 mM acetate was 1114 inadvertently added a culture at 48 h NF, and we noticed that the cells went on to 1115 accumulate far larger LBs than non-boosted cells. In our earlier publication (19), the 1116 effects of boost were monitored by light and electron microscopy, where both 1117 cytoplasmic and chloroplast LB size was more strongly enhanced in *sta6* than in *cw15*. 1118 1119 We document here that the rate of acetate depletion from media boosted to 40 mM 1120 acetate is unchanged from the rate of depletion at 20 mM acetate (Fig. 4), countering 1121 hypotheses that the boosted cells simply take up additional acetate for fatty-acid and 1122 hence TAG biosynthesis. 1123 1124 That said, there apparently occurs at least a pulse of acetate entry when the boost is 1125 administered, as documented by two events: 1) 229 flagella-related genes are 1126 transiently up-regulated in expression in *cw15*, the classic response to the acetic-acid-1127 mediated "pH shock" used to deflagellate *C. reinhardtii* cells (2); and 2) ~1300 additional 1128 genes are either up- or down-regulated in expression ≥2-fold following boost in both 1129 strains (Table 1), most quickly returning to pre-boost levels. Only a few of the up-1130 regulated genes encode enzymes in pathways for starch or lipid biosynthesis (Tables 2- 1131 5), but many encode proteins involved in nitrogen uptake and scavenging (Table S6) 1132 and in pathways of central carbon metabolism (Tables 6, 7 and S5).

1107 biosynthetic program, encoded in the *C. reinhardtii* genome, which is not called upon in

| 1149 | to N-deprivation, but it is accompanied by a smaller endowment of TAG in non-boosted          |
| 1150 | cells.                                                                                        |
| 1151 |                                                                                               |
| 1152 | We then noticed, in analyzing the 875 sta6 genes whose transcription is down-regulated        |
| 1153 | by acetate boost, a set of 64 genes encoding proteins that are expected to participate in     |
| 1154 | protein quality control and autophagocytic processes, including proteasome subunits,          |
| 1155 | chaperones, heat-shock proteins, cyclophilins, and proteases (Table 9). Moreover, 22 of       |
| 1156 | these genes show a sharp increase in expression in sta6 during the day immediately            |
| 1157 | prior to boost (Table S9), where an additional 20 non-annotated genes also show this          |
| 1158 | pattern as well and may represent additional members of the cohort. Hence expression          |

1159 of a sizable autophagy-related gene subset is either simply down-regulated by boost or 1160 else first stimulated at 48 h NF and the stimulation then aborted by boost. In both sets of 1161 experiments, *cw15* is less responsive to the proposed "autophagy signal" than is *sta6*, 1162 possibly because it is less stressed due to its starch reserves. These data suggest that 1163 the boost is able, for unknown reasons, to signal to *sta6* that it is not necessary to 1164 pursue the autophagy program, thereby enabling the cells to follow the path to full 1165 obesity.

<u>Table 4</u> Expression profiles (RPKM) of genes participating in TAG biosynthesis. Green highlight: boost addition; PB, post-boost. Yellow highlights: time points of maximum transcripts during  $0 \rightarrow 48$  h NF. Orange highlights: genes increasing expression  $\geq$ 2-fold relative to 48 h NF levels following acetate boost. Purple highlights: genes decreasing expression  $\geq$ 2-fold relative to 48 h NF levels following acetate boost. Blue highlights: genes showing strong differential expression between *sta6* and *cw15*.
